MIT Technology Review
Understanding the thermal ceiling in portable power
2026-09-09

Understanding the thermal ceiling in portable power

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Plug a phone into a modern charger and the first 10 minutes are impressive. The next 20 are not. This is not a defect. It’s the connected device protecting itself. As temperature rises during charging, a smartphone's battery management system reduces the current it will accept, because heat accelerates the chemical degradation that permanently reduces battery capacity. The charger may be capable of delivering more, but the device simply stops taking it. For anyone building products in the portable power category, this creates an uncomfortable gap between specification and experience. A device rated at 25 watts is accurate in the sense that it can deliver 25 watts. Whether it delivers 25 watts for the duration of a charge is a different question, and one the specification does not answer. The gap matters commercially because it is invisible at the point of purchase and obvious in use. Consumers compare wattage figures on packaging. They don’t compare thermal curves, because thermal curves are not published publicly. The result is a category where products differentiate on a number that describes peak output rather than sustained output, and where the actual user experience of two products with identical specifications can diverge substantially. This is particularly acute in magnetic wireless charging. Inductive power transfer generates heat at both the transmitting and receiving coils, and the magnetic attachment that makes these products convenient also places the heat source in direct contact with the device it is charging. Convenience and thermal performance are working against each other by design. The industry's response for the past several years has been materials science. Graphite sheets, thermal interface materials, conductive housings, and heat-spreading layers have all improved how efficiently accumulated heat moves away from the source. Each generation has been incrementally better than the last. But passive dissipation has a structural limitation: it can only move heat that has already been generated, and only as fast as the surrounding air will accept it. In a sealed, pocket-sized enclosure, that ceiling arrives quickly. Improving the materials slows the rate of temperature rise. It does not prevent the temperature rise. The alternative is active thermal management, which is standard in stationary electronics and largely absent from portable ones for reasons that are easy to understand. Fans add volume, weight, moving parts, and noise. In a product category defined by portability, each of those is a meaningful cost. At Anker, which manufactures charging and power products, engineering teams spent the past several development cycles working on whether that tradeoff could be made acceptable rather than eliminated. The approach involves several interacting systems: a micro centrifugal fan, dual airflow channels routed to avoid interference with the magnetic array, a three-layer graphene heat-spreading layer, and a control algorithm that modulates fan speed based on real-time temperature and battery state rather than running at a fixed rate. The result is that the Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro has become the world's fastest and coolest wireless power bank. In internal testing, at 77 °F (25 °C) ambient, the back of the power bank stays below 96.8 °F (36 °C) throughout wireless charging, 21.6 °F (12 °C) below the international standard limit of 118.4 °F (48 °C), for a comfortable grip. Comparable magnetic power banks in the same testing typically reached 113 °F (45 °C) or higher within 20 minutes. The functional consequence is that the connected device does not reach the threshold at which it begins reducing charge acceptance, so 25 watts of Qi2.2 magnetic wireless charging is delivered as a working rate rather than an opening rate. In practice, an iPhone 17 Pro reaches 50% charge in 25 minutes. The Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro’s premium performance in both charging speed and thermal management is certified by SGS, an independent testing and certification company. The same principle applies in reverse. Recharging a power bank generates heat too, which is why devices in this category are often slow to recharge, leaving users with an empty accessory at the moment they need it. Active cooling during input allows the unit to accept 45 watts and reach 80% in 52 minutes. There is a broader pattern here worth naming, because it is not unique to charging. When a category improves along a single axis for long enough, the constraint usually migrates somewhere else. Charging spent a decade optimizing power delivery. Power delivery is now, for most practical purposes, solved: the electronics can supply more energy than the receiving device is willing to accept. The binding constraint moved to thermal management, and the industry continued optimizing the axis it had always optimized, because that is the axis the specifications describe. Recognizing when a constraint has moved is difficult precisely because the old metric keeps improving. Wattage figures have continued to climb. Products have continued to get faster on paper. The measurement stayed valid while quietly ceasing to describe the thing users experience. For product organizations, the practical question is whether their specifications still measure the constraint or merely measure the capability. The two align until the constraint shifts and specifications rarely shift with it. A second implication follows from the first. If sustained performance differs meaningfully from peak performance, and if only peak performance is disclosed, then buyers cannot evaluate the products in front of them. This is one reason Anker is adding displays on charging products. The Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro shows real-time power, temperature, battery level, and estimated time remaining. Some of that is user convenience. But some of it is a Anker stating a deliberate position—this category deserves to have the complete and accurate data made transparent to all. Anker expects independent reviewers to test these claims and considers our internal numbers to be the correct outcome. The gap between specification and experience closes faster when the experience is measurable. The Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro will be available in the U.S. on September 17, 2026. MIT Technology Review
What OpenAI’s latest controversy tells us about the future of math
2026-09-09

What OpenAI’s latest controversy tells us about the future of math

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
OpenAI’s latest mathematical milestone has quickly become mired in controversy. Today, the company announced that its agents have solved one of the Millennium Prize Problems, some of the most important open problems in mathematics. Under normal circumstances, that solution would be a huge feather in OpenAI’s cap. But the announcement has been overshadowed by accusations that OpenAI used NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster’s and Anthropic employee Levent Alpöge’s AI-assisted work on the problem as a jumping-off point and failed to credit them. OpenAI has denied the accusations. It remains uncertain if OpenAI’s models made use of the work completed by Buckmaster and Alpöge, though Sébastien Bubeck, a member of the technical staff at OpenAI, said in a press briefing that the team was inspired to pursue the problem after hearing a rumor about Buckmaster and Alpöge’s efforts. But whether or not OpenAI’s models took advantage of Buckmaster and Alpöge’s research, this episode may mark a turning point in the history of mathematics. AI models now seem essential for making progress on the most important mathematical problems of our time, and solving them may demand resources only available at a couple of frontier AI companies, which often defy the norms of academic collaboration that undergird most mathematical progress. If that’s the future we are headed for, it is unclear how human mathematicians will fit into it. The problem that OpenAI claims to have solved is known as the Navier–Stokes existence and smoothness problem. It is one of seven Millennium Prize Problems selected by the Clay Mathematics Institute in 2000. Solutions come with a one million dollar prize; before today, only one other Millennium Prize Problem had been solved. The Navier–Stokes problem concerns a set of equations that describes how fluids, such as water and air, flow over time. The equations are widely used in the field of fluid dynamics, and they have proven powerful, but physicists and mathematicians didn’t understand them completely. In particular, it was unknown until today whether the equations might, under some conditions, break down and predict an impossible state of affairs—such as a fluid having infinite velocity. On Monday, NYU’s Buckmaster posted a proof on the social media site Mastodon showing that a simplified version of the Navier–Stokes equations can indeed break down—a major step forward on the Millennium Problem. He and Alpöge had worked on the problem for almost a year, using publicly available models from both OpenAI and Anthropic. Then today, OpenAI presented a proof showing that the full Navier–Stokes equations can break down as well. The proof was obtained using an internal model that dramatically outperforms the already-impressive Astra model, which was only released last week. The company says it does not plan to claim the million-dollar prize for solving the problem. These mathematical achievements are indisputably impressive, but they have attracted far less attention than the controversy about their origins. Along with the proof, Buckmaster posted a document detailing his interactions with OpenAI employees after he heard rumors about their work and reached out to one of them. According to him, OpenAI employees presented two possibilities to him: Either he and Alpöge could post their work and OpenAI would post their Navier-Stokes solution the following day, or he could work with OpenAI on a Navier-Stokes paper that excluded Alpöge from authorship, due to his affiliation with Anthropic, OpenAI’s biggest rival. Buckmaster also wrote that he asked the employees whether the agents had obtained access to transcripts of the work that he and Alpöge had done with OpenAI models, which they denied; and whether OpenAI models had been trained on those transcripts, to which they offered no response. MIT Technology Review reached out to Buckmaster for comment, but didn’t hear back before publication. The clear implication of the document is that OpenAI’s models somehow made use of Buckmaster and Alpöge’s work. That scenario is plausible on its face. The Buckmaster/Alpöge and OpenAI proofs both make use of an approach to the Navier-Stokes problem pioneered by the mathematicians Diego Córdoba and Luis Martínez-Zoroa. According to Javier Gómez-Serrano, a mathematics professor at Brown University, this approach was one of several that was thought to hold promise for solving the Navier-Stokes problem. So, while it’s by no means impossible that both teams could have arrived at this approach independently, it’s also conceivable that Buckmaster and Alpöge’s work could have influenced OpenAI’s. In the press briefing, Mark Chen, OpenAI’s chief research officer, again denied that any agents or OpenAI employees accessed Buckmaster and Alpöge’s transcripts—but given what has been revealed about the Hugging Face hack, it’s clear that OpenAI is not always entirely aware of what its agents are doing. If OpenAI’s models did train on Buckmaster and Alpöge’s work, or if its agents somehow gained access to it, then the company’s failure to track down the truth and assign those researchers appropriate credit reflects poorly on it. But there might be a thin silver lining to that version of the story for mathematicians, because it would suggest that the hard work of two humans, one of whom is a prominent expert on Navier-Stokes, was essential to the agents’ ability to solve the Millennium Problem. Experts have long identified “research taste,” or the ability to choose promising research questions and directions, as a major obstacle for AI in science and mathematics. If the OpenAI agents did indeed choose to follow the Córdoba–Martínez-Zoroa approach because Buckmaster and Alpöge had done the same, then human research taste played an essential role in OpenAI’s success. Even so, the bigger picture here is sobering. The progress that Buckmaster and Alpöge made over almost a year of collaboration with publicly available models speaks to the promise of human–AI collaboration. But they were not able to achieve a full solution. Meanwhile, OpenAI brute-forced a solution in a few days using an internal model, and their successful solution came at an astronomical cost: In the press briefing, Bubeck and Chen said the team was only able to solve the problem by running about 10,000 agents concurrently, at a cost of millions of dollars. Over the past few months, I’ve heard from several researchers that mathematicians are becoming depressed, and it’s not difficult to see why. Mathematics is quickly becoming the province of frontier AI companies with impressive internal-only models, money to burn, and a lack of collaborative spirit. “Whether AI companies will decide to spend their money on doing one thing or another, I truly don’t know,” says Gómez-Serrano. “What is clear is that very few mathematicians will have resources of that scale.” If OpenAI and Anthropic keep striving for more and more impressive mathematical accolades, there might not be any open problems left for human mathematicians outside of those companies to wrestle with. That would dramatically change the field of mathematics. Last week, UCLA mathematician Terence Tao wrote a Mastodon thread describing how important mistakes, wrong directions, and incomplete solutions are for the field. “In most cases in pure mathematics, the problems are posed not because we desperately want the solution to these problems in and of themselves, but because we have seen from past experience that human-directed efforts to solve these problems tend to spur further development of the field,” Tao wrote. “Prematurely solving the problem by purely AI-powered methods—particularly without full transparency into the solution process—can contaminate this process to the point where it actually becomes a net negative for the progress of mathematics as a whole.” Humans might take longer than agents to solve mathematical problems, but in the process, they uncover new mathematical approaches and ideas that might inspire their peers and even birth their own subfields. But when AI agents solve those problems instead—and when private companies keep the agents’ wrong turns from public view—those benefits disappear. It remains to be seen what else will vanish in the process. It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. AI doesn’t just learn stereotypes from its training. It can cook up new ones, too. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. In a new interview, the billionaire philanthropist sounds an alarm on the urgency of getting our AI policies in order. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. MIT Technology Review
This founder is teaching chips how to recycle (their energy)
2026-09-08

This founder is teaching chips how to recycle (their energy)

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Throughout the history of the computer chip, engineers have treated waste heat as an inevitable cost of a calculation. Hannah Earley, however, thinks it’s a design choice. Earley, 31, is cofounder and chief technology officer of Vaire Computing, a startup building chips that recycle energy usually thrown away as heat—a strategy known as reversible computing. Ultimately, she thinks, this approach could help make data centers (and our laptops and phones) much more energy efficient. When conventional computer chips perform calculations, they erase the information they no longer need along the way, dissipating energy as heat in the process. Earley compares the approach to racing through a city only to pump the brakes at every intersection: The car loses momentum and must burn more fuel to accelerate again. Reversible computing aims to keep the momentum going—instead of erasing information from the intermediate steps in a calculation, the circuit retains it, making it possible to run the computation backward and recover some of the energy. While the idea was first proposed more than 50 years ago, it proved impractical to implement with existing transistors and circuits. Earley, though, has completely rethought the hardware needed to make energy recovery work. She designed a patent-pending type of resonator—a microscopic chip component that stores recovered energy for later reuse. “It’s really a glorified pendulum,” she says. Last year, Vaire announced a key breakthrough: a chip with a resonator that recovered more energy than it lost, even after the energy needed to power the component was taken into account. For a subfield that has existed mostly in theory, the result was proof of life. “It’s clear they have something interesting,” says Igor Markov, a researcher in electronic design automation and a former professor at the University of Michigan, Ann Arbor. Still, he says, the technology is quite early stage; the company will need “a series of increasingly realistic and convincing demonstrations to attract the industry support needed for commercialization.” She gradually became convinced that the connection between information, energy, and heat could change computers forever. Earley’s journey into chip design started sooner than most. She began programming around the age of nine, starting with high-level coding for the web before digging into other programming languages like Perl and Java. She continued progressing to more and more abstract layers of computing, until she got all the way down to transistors. She eventually enrolled in a PhD program at the University of Cambridge under the computational biologist Gos Micklem. She started out studying how materials such as DNA could be used to perform calculations, but a few months in, Micklem sent her the 1999 PhD thesis of Michael Frank, a pioneer in reversible computing. Earley read it once, felt skeptical, read it again, and sat with it for a few weeks. She gradually became convinced that the connection between information, energy, and heat could change computers forever. The fascination completely redirected her PhD work. Earley studied the physical limits of computation and built software that could turn ordinary programs into reversible ones. “Eventually I wouldn’t let her put my name on any of her papers, because I felt that I couldn’t really stand up and give a proper talk about them,” Micklem recalls. “It was her stuff.” After completing her degree in 2021, Earley met Rodolfo Rosini, a technology entrepreneur and investor. The pair cofounded Vaire that same year, and the company has since raised more than $12 million, hired Frank as a senior scientist, and begun turning the vision of reversible computing into real hardware. Innovation, however, doesn’t happen overnight. During the winter of 2022 in Grinnell, Iowa, Earley spent weeks in her now-wife’s basement apartment as the wind chill outside reached roughly −40 °F, covering a whiteboard over and over again with schematics for the core piece of circuitry needed to make reversible logic work. By the time the design finally came together, after the couple had escaped the cold for Las Vegas, it felt less like an aha moment and more like a gradual wave of relief. “I’m not completely out of my depth,” she remembers feeling. Earley and her colleagues’ next challenge is making their drastically different chip fit into familiar devices and manufacturing systems. She believes that’s where the future lies—not in further refining existing chips but in rebuilding them from the ground up with an eye toward reversibility. “I want to tackle every part of how computers are built,” Earley says, “and rethink it in these terms.” It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. Some donor-conceived people are finding hundreds of siblings. An international cap on donations could help prevent that. AI doesn’t just learn stereotypes from its training. It can cook up new ones, too. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Google and NASA JPL release an AI model that maps methane plumes worldwide
2026-09-09

Google and NASA JPL release an AI model that maps methane plumes worldwide

The Next Web | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Google Research and NASA JPL have released a deep learning model that maps methane plumes globally at 60 metres, finding about 50% more than human analysts and 23,000 additional plumes, including at 24 of the 25 largest-emitting landfills. Europe’s Methane Regulation ordered a satellite monitoring tool and a super-emitter alert system, but it covers only oil, gas and coal. Google Research and NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory have released a deep learning model that maps methane plumes worldwide from space, Google Research said. It flags around 50% more plumes than human analysts, and 23,000 more than the existing product. The model is a vision transformer trained on 3.6 million simulated plumes. It reads raw spectra from EMIT, a NASA instrument on the International Space Station, at 60 metres per pixel. Europe already runs a version of this. The Copernicus atmosphere service switched on a methane hotspot explorer in February last year, built by Dutch researchers on Sentinel-5P data. The two are not doing the same job. Sentinel-5P covers the planet daily at roughly 5.5 by 3.5 kilometres per pixel, a region rather than a site. Sixty metres resolves a landfill cell. The paper reports plumes at 24 of the world’s 25 largest-emitting landfills, against 17 for the NASA product it replaces. UCLA built last year’s list of the worst sites from Carbon Mapper analysis of EMIT and Planet Labs data. Which is where Europe’s own rules stop short. The Methane Regulation applies to oil, gas and coal. It obliges the Commission to build a global monitoring tool on satellite data, and Article 31 alerts member states to super-emitting events. Waste is outside it. Prognos and the ifeu Institute estimated in January that municipal waste landfilled in Europe between 2022 and 2050 will release roughly 1.5 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent as methane. Europe’s high-resolution answer is still being built. AIRMO raised EUR 5M in March to launch its first satellite in early 2027, and the Copernicus CO2M mission is due in November of that year. What is available now costs nothing. The plume database sits on Earth Engine under a Creative Commons licence, the model is on Kaggle and the code on GitHub. The limit is where the instrument looks. EMIT was built to map mineral dust in arid regions, and it flies on a space station orbit inclined at 51.6 degrees. Europe was never the target. So the sharpest methane map in existence is a by-product of an American dust mission, given away. European Earth observation has the opposite trouble, as TNW has reported: plenty of data and too few buyers. A regulator wanting facility-level methane attribution tomorrow would download Google’s file, then find the emitters it resolves best are the ones the regulation does not reach. Google’s autumn roundup of its AI subscriptions leads on Gemini Spark handling web errands through Chrome for US subscribers, alongside voice tools, an image editor and a spreadsheet app builder that do reach Europe. Google has published an autumn roundup of its AI subscriptions on its own blog, adding voice features in Gmail, Docs and Keep, an image tool called Google Pics and a mini-app builder for Sheets. It leads on Gemini Spark, which will now handle web errands, edit photos and curate albums for Pro and Ultra subscribers in the United States. When Google expanded it to more than 160 countries in July, four markets were left out, PPC Land reported: the European Economic Area, Switzerland, the United Kingdom and Nigeria. Spark is the agentic part of the product. With permission it uses Chrome, saved passwords and logged-in accounts to schedule viewings or begin a flight booking, with human sign-off required for payments. The rest of the plan does reach Europe. Voice works with AI Plus, Pro and Ultra in Gmail and Keep, and with Pro and Ultra in Docs, while Pics and the Sheets canvas need Pro or Ultra. TNW noted two weeks ago that Google’s Gemini Live announcement named no European carve-out. This is the carve-out. So European subscribers buy the same tiers, at the same prices, and do not get the feature the tiers are being sold on. Google AI Pro is $19.99 a month and Plus is $4.99. Apple has been here. Its rebuilt Siri will not launch in the EU with iOS 27, after the Commission rejected every proposal it made, including an intermediary layer and an 18-month rollout. Apple at least said why, blaming demands that rival assistants receive the same system access as its own. Google has said nothing. The silence is awkward given what Google has already agreed to. Under its settlement with the Commission it must let rival assistants on Android reach comparable functionality by July 2027. Comparable to what is the question. An agent Google does not sell in Europe is one its rivals here cannot be measured against, and the remedy that came out of the Digital Markets Act proceedings was built to open exactly that comparison. Abu Dhabi says $100bn of AI capital sits in its financial centre. Its own announcement cannot decide whether that money is held there or spent.
2026-09-09

Abu Dhabi says $100bn of AI capital sits in its financial centre. Its own announcement cannot decide whether that money is held there or spent.

The Next Web | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Abu Dhabi Global Market reported more than $100bn in AI-focused capital in its first-half results, alongside 54% growth in assets under management. The same release describes that sum as deployed from the centre in one place and held by entities in another, which are not equivalent claims. Roughly half the total is MGX, the state-backed fund that closed at $49bn in July, and the category also covers an AI reinsurance agreement. Abu Dhabi Global Market published its first-half results on 8 September, reporting assets under management up 54% year on year and a workforce approaching 50,000. The headline claim is more than $100bn in AI-focused capital. Mohammed Sergie reported the figure for Semafor, framing it as money the centre now holds. ADGM’s own document supports two different readings. The summary bullet says $100bn in AI-focused capital is now deployed from ADGM by institutions established there. The body of the same release says the sum is held by entities established in the centre. Those are not equivalent statements. Deployed capital has been spent on something, while held capital may be sitting in a fund waiting for a target. The distinction matters at this size. One version describes $100bn of completed AI investment, and the other describes $100bn registered at an address. ADGM says the total is anchored by MGX, the state-backed vehicle licensed by its regulator. MGX closed its first fund at $49bn in July, above a $45bn target. That single vehicle accounts for roughly half the headline number. TNW covered that close when it happened, and the fund is a commitment pool rather than a record of purchases. A jurisdiction statistic dominated by one licensee is a statistic about that licensee. It describes where Abu Dhabi has chosen to domicile its own money. The release extends the category beyond MGX. It cites RIQ, which signed a memorandum of understanding with Swiss Re on AI-enabled reinsurance, and the investment firm Bluefive Capital. A reinsurance memorandum is a reasonable thing for a financial centre to celebrate. It is a different category of activity from buying compute or taking stakes in model developers. ADGM does not publish the breakdown. Without one, the $100bn cannot be checked against any particular set of assets. Assets under management rose 54%, which the release states without giving an absolute total. A percentage without a denominator cannot be compared with any rival centre. The underlying activity is real and does not depend on the headline number. Fund and asset managers rose to 190, funds managed from ADGM to 276, and financial services entities to 392, with Bain Capital, Blue Owl, Man Group, Barings and Capital Group among those establishing or expanding. Those are countable things. They are also less quotable than $100bn, which is why the larger figure travelled. The financial district is being expanded on a scale routinely compared to Hudson Yards. Mubadala and Aldar put the gross development value above AED 60bn, roughly $16.3bn. Gross development value is what the finished thing is expected to be worth, not what it costs to build. The two get used interchangeably, and they should not be. Abu Dhabi has money. What it does not fully control is access to the chips that make AI assets productive, and that access is decided in Washington. The strain is already visible at the emirate’s other AI champion. G42 has been weighing majority US ownership to protect its chip supply, having already dropped Huawei equipment and accepted American oversight of its data centres. MGX has taken positions in US frontier labs and infrastructure, including the $40bn Aligned Data Centers deal alongside BlackRock. Those assets are American, and the returns depend on export policy that Abu Dhabi does not write. Sovereign AI capital ends up in buildings full of hardware in specific places. Iran has threatened the Stargate data centre in Abu Dhabi. Chips have also become instruments of statecraft rather than ordinary exports. Washington has used Nvidia allocations as a term in a peace agreement. None of that shows up in an AUM growth rate. It is the risk attached to every dollar in the $100bn. ADGM could publish the split between committed and deployed capital, and say which entities make up the total. Both are ordinary disclosures for a centre seeking a place in the global top five. Until then the accurate description is narrower than the headline. Institutions licensed in Abu Dhabi have raised or allocated more than $100bn towards artificial intelligence, and how much of it has actually been spent is not public. Boston Dynamics spinoff looks to build robots for entertainment

The Next Web | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Dynamic Creatures, a Boston Dynamics spinoff, has launched to build robots for theme parks and retail, writing software and buying hardware from Unitree, AgiBot and Boston Dynamics. Europe’s Machinery Regulation covers AI safety functions from January 2027, and the French company that created this category was sold to a Shenzhen buyer last year. Dynamic Creatures, a Boston Dynamics spinoff, has launched out of stealth to build robots for theme parks and retail, Semafor reported. It writes the software and buys hardware from Unitree, AgiBot and Boston Dynamics. Chief executive Marc Theermann said safety has to be rethought “from the ground up“, because he wants robots people will be comfortable hugging. Boston Dynamics is not doing this itself. Character development “just isn’t our core space or area of expertise“, said Nikolas Noel, its vice-president of marketing and communications. The engineering problems are unglamorous. Whether a costume blocks a vision system or makes the robot overheat is the kind of thing this category brings. Nobody has said which parks, or when. Two of the three named hardware suppliers, Unitree and AgiBot, are Chinese. Boston Dynamics itself is majority owned by Hyundai. Europe already has a rulebook for the thing Theermann is describing. The Machinery Regulation replaces the machinery directive on 20 January 2027, and covers AI-based safety functions and machinery with self-evolving behaviour for the first time. Both categories need a notified body rather than a self-declaration. Specifications that limit how fast or how far a machine moves count as safety functions under it. A robot deciding how firmly to hold a child sits squarely inside them. Europe also used to own this category outright. Aldebaran, the Paris company behind Nao and Pepper, shipped about 20,000 and 17,000 of them into 70 countries, The Robot Report said. Aldebaran was founded in Paris in 2005. It filed for bankruptcy in February last year and was placed in receivership in June. In July, Maxvision of Shenzhen bought the core assets behind Nao and Pepper. So the entertainment robot is being rebuilt by an American software company on mostly Chinese hardware, in a category a French firm created and then sold to a Chinese buyer. UMA, founded by a former Tesla Optimus researcher, is building one there and says it will take Europe first. The rules arrive in January 2027, which is roughly when a robot built to be hugged would reach a European theme park. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Instacart says AI-built carts beat its $115 average basket. Shipt shipped the same feature and called it relief from mental load.
2026-09-09

Instacart says AI-built carts beat its $115 average basket. Shipt shipped the same feature and called it relief from mental load.

The Next Web | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Ask Shipt turns a plain-language request into a filled cart at a chosen retailer. Shipt and Instacart both launched AI assistants that build a grocery cart from a prompt or a photo on 9 September. Instacart said orders placed through its assistant contain more items than a typical order and exceed its $115 average basket, while Shipt’s announcement gives no equivalent figure and frames the product as relief from mental load. Neither company says whether paid placements can enter an assistant-built cart, or whether a shopper would be told. Shipt launched Ask Shipt on 9 September, an assistant in its app that turns a prompt, a recipe or a photo of a meal into a cart. Instacart released Clementine the same day, which does the same job. The Shelby Report set the two launches side by side, noting Kroger had already put an assistant across all its banners in July. Conversational cart building is now the fastest-moving front in grocery delivery. Instacart said orders placed through Clementine contain more items than its typical order and come in above its $115 average basket. That is the commercial case for the product, stated plainly by the company selling it. Shipt’s announcement contains no equivalent figure. It describes the assistant as relief from decision fatigue and from the mental load of feeding a family. Both framings can be true. An assistant that removes the effort of assembling twenty items also removes the friction that used to stop the twentieth going in. Grocery platforms are advertising businesses. Sponsored placements are keyword-targeted to what a shopper types into a search bar, which is precisely the surface these assistants replace. So the live question is whether a brand can pay to appear in a cart the assistant assembles. Neither company’s announcement addresses it, and neither says how a shopper would know if it happened. Shipt is an independently operated subsidiary of Target, which runs its own retail media business, Roundel. Instacart’s own enterprise marketing puts retail media at $69bn. A search bar puts the shopper in charge of the query and shows a labelled ad beside the result. An assistant chooses the items, and the reasoning for each choice sits inside a model. The FTC’s case against Amazon turns on exactly this gap, alleging the company described one set of auction rules while running another. Amazon’s defence is that its reserve prices were never predictable by anyone, including Amazon. That case concerns advertisers, who at least have dashboards. A shopper handed a finished cart has no comparable way to audit it. Shipt reaches 80% of the US population through more than 100 retail partners. Instacart works with more than 2,200 retail banners covering close to 100,000 stores in North America. Instacart is also selling the technology to rivals. Its white-label Cart Assistant is live at several grocers, with Aldi’s US business among those still to come. Three weeks before this launch, Shipt put shopping experiences inside ChatGPT and Claude. That is the opposite bet to building your own assistant. One says the journey starts in a general assistant and Shipt is the fulfilment layer underneath. The other says the customer opens the Shipt app first. Running both is rational while nobody knows which wins. Amazon has already moved its assistant into the search bar, and Google is building a universal cart that spans its products. Every large platform is now trying to own the container rather than the catalogue. A court has already ruled on whether an outside agent may shop on somebody else’s storefront. Shipt sits in an unusual position in that fight. It is a fulfilment layer for other retailers, a Target asset, and now a destination assistant competing with the ones it plugs into. An assistant that assembles a basket is a recommender system with a checkout button attached. The European Commission is due to table its Digital Fairness Act, which covers personalisation and recommendation practices. Neither of these assistants is live in the EU, and Shipt does not operate there. The rules will still shape what an AI cart builder has to disclose, because the firms designing them are building for a global market. Regulators on both sides of the Atlantic are already looking at interface design in shopping apps. A cart the shopper did not assemble is an interface question before it is an AI one. The answer is one sentence from each company. Do sponsored or otherwise paid placements enter assistant-built carts, and are they labelled when they do? Neither company has been asked it publicly, and neither has volunteered an answer. Until one does, the honest description is that nobody outside either company knows what is in the cart or why. Apple’s first foldable is the iPhone Duo, and it costs $1,999
2026-09-09

Apple’s first foldable is the iPhone Duo, and it costs $1,999

The Next Web | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
iPhone Duo’s 5.4-inch outer display, left, and its 7.6-inch inner display. Apple has made a foldable iPhone. It is called the iPhone Duo, it opens to 7.6 inches, and it starts at $1,999. The price is the news. Analysts expected $2,500. Samsung, which has had this category almost to itself for seven years, sells its Z Fold8 Ultra at $2,099. Apple arrived last and priced below the incumbent. Apple unveiled the Duo on Wednesday. Pre-orders open on 16 October in more than 70 countries, with the phone arriving on 23 October and in 28 more markets from 30 October. Storage runs from 256GB to 2TB. There are two colours, star white and night sky. Closed, the outer display is 5.4 inches and carries 90% of the screen area of an iPhone 18 Pro. Open, the inner display is 7.6 inches and 50% larger than an iPhone 18 Pro Max. Both share an aspect ratio, which is what makes content scale between them. Here is the detail worth pausing on. The Duo runs the A20 Pro, the same chip as the iPhone 18 Pro announced the same morning. That phone starts at $1,199. So the $800 difference buys the hinge, the second display, the titanium and the dual battery. It does not buy faster silicon. Apple pairs it with a custom vapour chamber and a chip package it says is inspired by its M-series work, claiming up to 35% better sustained performance than the iPhone 17 Pro. A new display engine drives both screens at once. We reported in August that IDC expected $2,500 and more than 10 million units in the first year. Nabila Popal, a senior research director there, described the buyers as a very elite set of consumers. Apple came in $500 under that. Our own August preview put the range at $2,000 to $2,500, so the Duo landed at the very bottom of it. Against Samsung the gap runs the other way. IDC expected Apple to price $400 above the Z Fold8 Ultra. It went $100 below. That is unusual in this particular year. Memory prices have pushed component costs up all year, and Omdia argued in August that phones had undergone a structural repricing. This is the first product launch under John Ternus, who became chief executive on 1 September, and he took the Duo himself. “iPhone Duo is the most transformational change to iPhone since the original. With an entirely new design and intuitive experiences, it shows what’s possible when hardware and software are engineered together and redefines what it means to use a foldable phone,” he said. He is a hardware engineer, and the Duo is a hardware argument. Every foldable has failed on the fold. Apple has spent its engineering budget accordingly. The hinge uses more than 100 components to control opening and closing and to support the centre of the display. Its cover is 3D-printed titanium, micro-blasted against the mirror-polished grade 5 titanium body. Support ribs stiffen the structure, and ceramic fibre inserts in the antenna splits add rigidity. The display stack is the other half. A nano-texture finish cuts glare and reflections and, Apple says, minimises how visible the crease is. Under a scratch-resistant coating sits a custom polymer cover Apple claims is up to 40% stiffer than industry alternatives. High-strength glass sits above and below the folding panel. The adhesives are the clever part. Apple says they let the layers glide past one another like the pages of a book, relieving bend stress rather than resisting it. A titanium plate at the bottom adds durability. Ceramic Shield 2 on the front resists scratches three times better than the previous generation. Ceramic Shield covers the back. The rating is IP68. Face ID does not appear. Touch ID is built into the side button instead, working open or closed, and the Duo can also be unlocked with an Apple Watch. Our preview predicted that, reasoning that a folding phone has nowhere sensible for the Face ID array. The FaceTime camera now sits under the inner display and hides when not in use. Two things land differently here. Start with the energy label, which every smartphone sold in the EU has carried since June 2025. It shows a repairability class, a battery endurance figure and a rating for surviving repeated drops. Regulation 2023/1670 exempts displays that roll and unroll. It does not exempt ones that fold. So the Duo faces the same 800-cycle battery minimum and seven-year spare parts obligation as any handset, and Apple must publish a durability grade for the most expensive phone it has made. Its iPhone 17 Pro Max is class C for repairability and class B for repeated free fall. Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 7 beat it on both free fall and battery cycles. The second is Siri AI, which footnote 3 says “will not be available initially in the EU.” Apple gives no reason and no date. That matches the iPhone 18 Pro and both new watches, announced the same day. There is a high-energy battery on each side, run as one through Apple silicon and what the company calls energy rebalancing algorithms. Apple quotes up to 31 hours of video playback on the inner display, 44 hours on the outer, and 24 hours of use per charge with both used equally. Charging reaches 50% in around 20 minutes wired, or 30 minutes on MagSafe or Qi2. The Duo is eSIM-only worldwide, with no physical SIM tray in any market. Apple says that is what freed the space. Apple rebuilt the interface rather than stretching it. Lock Screen controls, the Dock and app navigation all move to the side, freeing vertical space. Open, the inner display shows two Home Screen pages beside a vertically scrolling Today View. The status bar shrinks to a circular element in a corner. The Dynamic Island now runs vertically down the side of both displays. Split View puts two apps side by side on an iPhone for the first time. Users can open two windows of the same app, swap quickly between them, and save pairs to return to. StandBy now works on either display even when the phone is not charging. Netflix, Zoom and Slack have already adapted. The Duo is also the first iPhone to take an Apple Pencil, on either display, arriving later this year. The rear system pairs a 48MP Fusion Main camera, with zero shutter lag and an integrated optical-quality 2x telephoto, with the same 48MP Fusion Ultra Wide as the iPhone 18 Pro. It shoots 4K120 in Dolby Vision. The new ideas come from having a screen on the back. Duo Preview shows subjects a live preview on the outer display so they can check their own framing. Duo FaceTime lets people beside you join a call on that screen instead of crowding in. Smart Take uses on-device AI to fire the shutter when it sees people posing. Kid Cue plays Peanuts animations to get children to look up. Camera Control returns, alongside a new Siri Camera mode that lets Siri see what the user sees. Whether the hinge survives two years rather than a demo. What durability class Apple publishes on the European label, and how it compares with Samsung’s. And whether $1,999 was about cost or about share. Apple has raised prices on almost everything else this year. On the one product where it had no incumbent position, it did the opposite.
